The Life of Henry V

William Shakespeare — EMOTHE0182_TheLifeOfHenryV

Act I

[Prologue]

Enter [Chorus as] Prologue.

CHORUS

35Oh, for a muse of fire, that would ascend
36The brightest heaven of invention,
37A kingdom for a stage, princes to act,
38And monarchs to behold the swelling scene.
39Then should the warlike Harry, like himself,
40Assume the port of Mars, and at his heels,
41Leashed in like hounds, should famine, sword, and fire
42Crouch for employment. But pardon, gentles all,
43The flat unraisèd spirits that hath dared,
44On this unworthy scaffold, to bring forth
45So great an object. Can this cockpit hold
46The vasty fields of France? Or may we cram
47Within this wooden O the very casques
48That did affright the air at Agincourt?
49Oh, pardon, since a crooked figure may
50Attest in little place a million,
51And let us, ciphers to this great account,
52On your imaginary forces work.
53Suppose within the girdle of these walls
54Are now confined two mighty monarchies,
55Whose high, uprearèd, and abutting fronts
56The perilous narrow ocean parts asunder.
57Piece out our imperfections with your thoughts:
58Into a thousand parts divide one man,
59And make imaginary puissance.
60Think, when we talk of horses, that you see them
61Printing their proud hooves i'th'receiving earth.
62For 'tis your thoughts that now must deck our kings,
63Carry them here and there, jumping o'er times,
64Turning th'accomplishment of many years
65Into an hourglass: for the which supply,
66Admit me Chorus to this history,
67Who, prologue-like, your humble patience pray
68Gently to hear, kindly to judge our play.

Exit.

1.1

Enter [the Archbishop of] Canterbury and [the Bishop of] Ely.

CANTERBURY

69My lord, I'll tell you, that self bill is urged
70Which in th'eleventh year of the last king's reign
71Was like, and had indeed against us passed,
72But that the scambling and unquiet time
73Did push it out of further question.

ELY

74But how, my lord, shall we resist it now?

CANTERBURY

75It must be thought on. If it pass against us,
76We lose the better half of our possession,
77For all the temporal lands which men devout
78By testament have given to the Church
79Would they strip from us, being valued thus:
80As much as would maintain, to the king's honor,
81Full fifteen earls and fifteen hundred knights,
82Six thousand and two hundred good esquires,
83And to relief of lazars and weak age
84Of indigent faint souls past corporal toil,
85A hundred almshouses, right well supplied;
86And to the coffers of the king beside,
87A thousand pounds by th'year. Thus runs the bill.

ELY

88This would drink deep.

CANTERBURY

This would drink deep. 'Twould drink the cup and all.

ELY

89But what prevention?

CANTERBURY

90The king is full of grace and fair regard.

ELY

91And a true lover of the holy Church.

CANTERBURY

92The courses of his youth promised it not.
93The breath no sooner left his father's body,
94But that his wildness, mortified in him,
95Seemed to die too. Yea, at that very moment,
96Consideration like an angel came,
97And whipped th'offending Adam out of him,
98Leaving his body as a paradise,
99T'envelop and contain celestial spirits.
100Never was such a sudden scholar made,
101Never came reformation in a flood
102With such a heady currence scouring faults,
103Nor never hydra-headed willfulness
104So soon did lose his seat, and all at once,
105As in this king.

ELY

As in this king. We are blessèd in the change.

CANTERBURY

106Hear him but reason in divinity,
107And, all-admiring, with an inward wish
108You would desire the king were made a prelate.
109Hear him debate of commonwealth affairs,
110You would say it hath been all in all his study.
111List his discourse of war, and you shall hear
112A fearful battle rendered you in music.
113Turn him to any cause of policy,
114The Gordian knot of it he will unloose,
115Familiar as his garter; that when he speaks,
116The air, a chartered libertine, is still,
117And the mute wonder lurketh in men's ears
118To steal his sweet and honeyed sentences,
119So that the art and practic part of life
120Must be the mistress to this theoric.
121Which is a wonder how his grace should glean it,
122Since his addiction was to courses vain,
123His companies unlettered, rude, and shallow,
124His hours filled up with riots, banquets, sports,
125And never noted in him any study,
126Any retirement, any sequestration
127From open haunts and popularity.

ELY

128The strawberry grows underneath the nettle,
129And wholesome berries thrive and ripen best
130Neighbored by fruit of baser quality;
131And so the prince obscured his contemplation
132Under the veil of wildness, which no doubt
133Grew like the summer grass, fastest by night,
134Unseen, yet crescive in his faculty.

CANTERBURY

135It must be so, for miracles are ceased,
136And therefore we must needs admit the means
137How things are perfected.

ELY

How things are perfected. But my good lord,
138How now for mitigation of this bill
139Urged by the commons? Doth his majesty
140Incline to it or no?

CANTERBURY

Incline to it or no? He seems indifferent,
141Or rather swaying more upon our part,
142Than cherishing th'exhibitors against us;
143For I have made an offer to his majesty,
144Upon our spiritual convocation,
145And in regard of causes now in hand,
146Which I have opened to his grace at large,
147As touching France, to give a greater sum
148Than ever at one time the clergy yet
149Did to his predecessors part withal.

ELY

150How did this offer seem received, my lord?

CANTERBURY

151With good acceptance of his majesty,
152Save that there was not time enough to hear,
153As I perceived his grace would fain have done,
154The severals and unhidden passages
155Of his true titles to some certain dukedoms,
156And generally to the crown and seat of France
157Derived from Edward, his great-grandfather.

ELY

158What was th'impediment that broke this off?

CANTERBURY

159The French ambassador upon that instant
160Craved audience; and the hour, I think, is come
161To give him hearing. Is it four o'clock?

ELY

162It is.

CANTERBURY

163Then go we in to know his embassy,
164Which I could with a ready guess declare
165Before the Frenchman speak a word of it.

ELY

166I'll wait upon you, and I long to hear it.

Exeunt.

[1.2]

Enter the King, Humphrey [Duke of Gloucester], Bedford, Clarence, Warwick, Westmorland, and Exeter[, with attendants].

KING HENRY

167Where is my gracious lord of Canterbury?

EXETER

168Not here in presence.

KING HENRY

Not here in presence. Send for him, good uncle.

[Exit attendant.]

WESTMORLAND

169Shall we call in th'ambassador, my liege?

KING HENRY

170Not yet, my cousin. We would be resolved,
171Before we hear him, of some things of weight
172That task our thoughts concerning us and France.

Enter [the] two Bishops[, Canterbury and Ely].

CANTERBURY

173God and his angels guard your sacred throne
174And make you long become it.

KING HENRY

And make you long become it. Sure we thank you.
175My learnèd lord, we pray you to proceed,
176And justly and religiously unfold
177Why the law Salic, that they have in France,
178Or should or should not bar us in our claim.
179And God forbid, my dear and faithful lord,
180That you should fashion, wrest, or bow your reading,
181Or nicely charge your understanding soul
182With opening titles miscreate, whose right
183Suits not in native colors with the truth.
184For God doth know how many now in health
185Shall drop their blood in approbation
186Of what your reverence shall incite us to.
187Therefore take heed how you impawn our person,
188How you awake our sleeping sword of war;
189We charge you in the name of God, take heed.
190For never two such kingdoms did contend
191Without much fall of blood, whose guiltless drops
192Are every one a woe, a sore complaint
193'Gainst him whose wrongs gives edge unto the swords
194That makes such waste in brief mortality.
195Under this conjuration speak, my lord,
196For we will hear, note, and believe in heart
197That what you speak is in your conscience washed
198As pure as sin with baptism.

CANTERBURY

199Then hear me, gracious sovereign, and you peers
200That owe your selves, your lives and services
201To this imperial throne. There is no bar
202To make against your highness' claim to France
203But this, which they produce from Pharamond:
204"In terram Salicam mulieres ne succedant" —
205"No woman shall succeed in Salic land" —
206Which Salic land the French unjustly gloss
207To be the realm of France, and Pharamond
208The founder of this law and female bar.
209Yet their own authors faithfully affirm
210That the land Salic is in Germany,
211Between the floods of Saale and of Elbe,
212Where Charles the Great, having subdued the Saxons,
213There left behind and settled certain French
214Who, holding in disdain the German women
215For some dishonest manners of their life,
216Established then this law: to wit, no female
217Should be inheritrix in Salic land,
218Which Salic, as I said, 'twixt Elbe and Saale,
219Is at this day in Germany, called Meissen.
220Then doth it well appear the Salic law
221Was not devisèd for the realm of France,
222Nor did the French possess the Salic land
223Until four hundred one-and-twenty years
224After defunction of King Pharamond,
225Idly supposed the founder of this law,
226Who died within the year of our redemption
227Four hundred twenty-six; and Charles the Great
228Subdued the Saxons and did seat the French
229Beyond the river Saale in the year
230Eight hundred five. Besides, their writers say
231King Pepin, which deposèd Childeric,
232Did as heir general, being descended
233Of Blithild, which was daughter to King Chlothar,
234Make claim and title to the crown of France.
235Hugh Capet also, who usurped the crown
236Of Charles the Duke of Lorraine, sole heir male
237Of the true line and stock of Charles the Great,
238To find his title with some shows of truth —
239Though in pure truth it was corrupt and naught —
240Conveyed himself as th'heir to th'lady Lingare,
241Daughter to Charlemagne, who was the son
242To Louis the emperor, and Louis the son
243Of Charles the Great. Also King Louis the Tenth,
244Who was sole heir to the usurper Capet,
245Could not keep quiet in his conscience
246Wearing the crown of France till satisfied
247That fair Queen Isabelle, his grandmother,
248Was lineal of the lady Ermengarde,
249Daughter to Charles the foresaid Duke of Lorraine,
250By the which marriage the line of Charles the Great
251Was reunited to the crown of France.
252So that, as clear as is the summer's sun,
253King Pepin's title and Hugh Capet's claim,
254King Louis his satisfaction, all appear
255To hold in right and title of the female;
256So do the kings of France unto this day,
257Howbeit they would hold up this Salic law
258To bar your highness claiming from the female,
259And rather choose to hide them in a net
260Than amply to embar their crooked titles
261Usurped from you and your progenitors.

KING HENRY

262May I with right and conscience make this claim?

CANTERBURY

263The sin upon my head, dread sovereign.
264For in the book of Numbers is it writ:
265"When the man dies, let the inheritance
266Descend unto the daughter." Gracious lord,
267Stand for your own. Unwind your bloody flag,
268Look back into your mighty ancestors.
269Go, my dread lord, to your great-grandsire's tomb,
270From whom you claim; invoke his warlike spirit,
271And your great-uncle's, Edward the Black Prince,
272Who on the French ground played a tragedy,
273Making defeat on the full power of France
274Whiles his most mighty father on a hill
275Stood smiling to behold his lion's whelp
276Forage in blood of French nobility.
277Oh, noble English, that could entertain
278With half their forces the full pride of France
279And let another half stand laughing by,
280All out of work and cold for action!

ELY

281Awake remembrance of these valiant dead,
282And with your puissant arm renew their feats.
283You are their heir, you sit upon their throne,
284The blood and courage that renownèd them
285Runs in your veins, and my thrice-puissant liege
286Is in the very May-morn of his youth,
287Ripe for exploits and mighty enterprises.

EXETER

288Your brother kings and monarchs of the earth
289Do all expect that you should rouse yourself
290As did the former lions of your blood.

WESTMORLAND

291They know your grace hath cause, and means, and might;
292So hath your highness. Never king of England
293Had nobles richer and more loyal subjects,
294Whose hearts have left their bodies here in England
295And lie pavilioned in the fields of France.

CANTERBURY

296Oh, let their bodies follow, my dear liege,
297With bloods and sword and fire to win your right.
298In aid whereof, we of the spiritualty
299Will raise your highness such a mighty sum
300As never did the clergy at one time
301Bring in to any of your ancestors.

KING HENRY

302We must not only arm t'invade the French,
303But lay down our proportions to defend
304Against the Scot, who will make road upon us
305With all advantages.

CANTERBURY

306They of those marches, gracious sovereign,
307Shall be a wall sufficient to defend
308Our inland from the pilfering borderers.

KING HENRY

309We do not mean the coursing snatchers only,
310But fear the main intendment of the Scot,
311Who hath been still a giddy neighbor to us.
312For you shall read that my great-grandfather
313Never went with his forces into France
314But that the Scot on his unfurnished kingdom
315Came pouring like the tide into a breach
316With ample and brim fullness of his force,
317Galling the gleanèd land with hot assays,
318Girding with grievous siege castles and towns,
319That England, being empty of defense,
320Hath shook and trembled at th'ill neighborhood.

CANTERBURY

321She hath been then more feared than harmed, my liege.
322For hear her but exampled by herself:
323When all her chivalry hath been in France
324And she a mourning widow of her nobles,
325She hath herself not only well defended,
326But taken and impounded as a stray
327The king of Scots, whom she did send to France
328To fill King Edward's fame with prisoner kings
329And make their chronicle as rich with praise
330As is the ooze and bottom of the sea
331With sunken wreck and sumless treasuries.

ELY

332But there's a saying very old and true:
333"If that you will France win,
334Then with Scotland first begin."
335For once the eagle England being in prey,
336To her unguarded nest the weasel Scot
337Comes sneaking, and so sucks her princely eggs,
338Playing the mouse in absence of the cat,
339To 'tame and havoc more than she can eat.

EXETER

340It follows then the cat must stay at home,
341Yet that is but a crushed necessity,
342Since we have locks to safeguard necessaries
343And pretty traps to catch the petty thieves.
344While that the armèd hand doth fight abroad,
345Th'advisèd head defends itself at home.
346For government, though high and low and lower
347Put into parts, doth keep in one consent,
348Congreeing in a full and natural close
349Like music.

CANTERBURY

Like music. Therefore doth heaven divide
350The state of man in divers functions,
351Setting endeavor in continual motion,
352To which is fixèd, as an aim or butt,
353Obedience. For so work the honeybees,
354Creatures that by a rule in nature teach
355The act of order to a peopled kingdom.
356They have a king, and officers of sorts,
357Where some like magistrates correct at home;
358Others like merchants venture trade abroad;
359Others, like soldiers armèd in their stings,
360Make boot upon the summer's velvet buds,
361Which pillage they with merry march bring home
362To the tent-royal of their emperor,
363Who, busied in his majesties, surveys
364The singing masons building roofs of gold,
365The civil citizens kneading up the honey,
366The poor mechanic porters crowding in
367Their heavy burdens at his narrow gate,
368The sad-eyed justice with his surly hum
369Delivering o'er to executors pale
370The lazy yawning drone. I this infer:
371That many things, having full reference
372To one consent, may work contrariously.
373As many arrows loosèd several ways
374Come to one mark, as many ways meet in one town,
375As many fresh streams meet in one salt sea,
376As many lines close in the dial's center,
377So may a thousand actions once afoot
378End in one purpose, and be all well borne
379Without defeat. Therefore to France, my liege.
380Divide your happy England into four,
381Whereof take you one quarter into France,
382And you withal shall make all Gallia shake.
383If we, with thrice such powers left at home,
384Cannot defend our own doors from the dog,
385Let us be worried, and our nation lose
386The name of hardiness and policy.

KING HENRY

387Call in the messengers sent from the dauphin.

[Exit attendant.]

388Now are we well resolved, and by God's help
389And yours, the noble sinews of our power,
390France being ours, we'll bend it to our awe,
391Or break it all to pieces. Or there we'll sit,
392Ruling in large and ample empery
393O'er France and all her almost kingly dukedoms,
394Or lay these bones in an unworthy urn,
395Tombless, with no remembrance over them.
396Either our history shall with full mouth
397Speak freely of our acts, or else our grave,
398Like Turkish mute, shall have a tongueless mouth,
399Not worshipped with a waxen epitaph. —

Enter Ambassadors of France.

400Now are we well prepared to know the pleasure
401Of our fair cousin dauphin, for we hear
402Your greeting is from him, not from the king.

AMBASSADOR

403May't please your majesty to give us leave
404Freely to render what we have in charge,
405Or shall we sparingly show you far off
406The dauphin's meaning and our embassy?

KING HENRY

407We are no tyrant, but a Christian king,
408Unto whose grace our passion is as subject
409As is our wretches fettered in our prisons.
410Therefore with frank and with uncurbèd plainness
411Tell us the dauphin's mind.

AMBASSADOR

Tell us the dauphin's mind. Thus, then, in few:
412Your highness, lately sending into France,
413Did claim some certain dukedoms in the right
414Of your great predecessor, King Edward the Third.
415In answer of which claim, the prince our master
416Says that you savor too much of your youth,
417And bids you be advised, there's naught in France
418That can be with a nimble galliard won;
419You cannot revel into dukedoms there.
420He therefore sends you, meeter for your spirit,
421This tun of treasure, and in lieu of this,
422Desires you let the dukedoms that you claim
423Hear no more of you. This the dauphin speaks.

KING HENRY

424What treasure, uncle?

EXETER

What treasure, uncle? Tennis balls, my liege.

KING HENRY

425We are glad the dauphin is so pleasant with us.
426His present and your pains we thank you for.
427When we have matched our rackets to these balls,
428We will in France, by God's grace, play a set
429Shall strike his father's crown into the hazard.
430Tell him he hath made a match with such a wrangler
431That all the courts of France will be disturbed
432With chases. And we understand him well,
433How he comes o'er us with our wilder days,
434Not measuring what use we made of them.
435We never valued this poor seat of England,
436And therefore living hence, did give ourself
437To barbarous license, as 'tis ever common
438That men are merriest when they are from home.
439But tell the dauphin I will keep my state,
440Be like a king, and show my sail of greatness
441When I do rouse me in my throne of France.
442For that I have laid by my majesty
443And plodded like a man for working days,
444But I will rise there with so full a glory
445That I will dazzle all the eyes of France,
446Yea, strike the dauphin blind to look on us.
447And tell the pleasant prince this mock of his
448Hath turned his balls to gunstones, and his soul
449Shall stand sore chargèd for the wasteful vengeance
450That shall fly with them: for many a thousand widows
451Shall this, his mock, mock out of their dear husbands,
452Mock mothers from their sons, mock castles down,
453And some are yet ungotten and unborn
454That shall have cause to curse the dauphin's scorn.
455But this lies all within the will of God,
456To whom I do appeal, and in whose name
457Tell you the dauphin I am coming on
458To venge me as I may, and to put forth
459My rightful hand in a well-hallowed cause.
460So get you hence in peace, and tell the dauphin
461His jest will savor but of shallow wit
462When thousands weep more than did laugh at it. —
463Convey them with safe conduct. — Fare you well.

Exeunt Ambassadors.

EXETER

464This was a merry message.

KING HENRY

465We hope to make the sender blush at it.
466Therefore, my lords, omit no happy hour
467That may give furtherance to our expedition,
468For we have now no thought in us but France,
469Save those to God, that run before our business.
470Therefore let our proportions for these wars
471Be soon collected, and all things thought upon
472That may with reasonable swiftness add
473More feathers to our wings, for, God before,
474We'll chide this dauphin at his father's door.
475Therefore let every man now task his thought
476That this fair action may on foot be brought.

Exeunt.

Act II

[2.0]

Flourish. Enter Chorus.

CHORUS

477Now all the youth of England are on fire
478And silken dalliance in the wardrobe lies.
479Now thrive the armorers, and honor's thought
480Reigns solely in the breast of every man.
481They sell the pasture now to buy the horse,
482Following the mirror of all Christian kings
483With wingèd heels, as English Mercuries.
484For now sits expectation in the air
485And hides a sword from hilts unto the point
486With crowns imperial, crowns and coronets
487Promised to Harry and his followers.
488The French, advised by good intelligence
489Of this most dreadful preparation,
490Shake in their fear, and with pale policy
491Seek to divert the English purposes.
492O England, model to thy inward greatness,
493Like little body with a mighty heart,
494What mightst thou do, that honor would thee do,
495Were all thy children kind and natural!
496But see, thy fault France hath in thee found out:
497A nest of hollow bosoms, which he fills
498With treacherous crowns; and three corrupted men,
499One, Richard, Earl of Cambridge, and the second
500Henry, Lord Scrope of Masham, and the third
501Sir Thomas Grey, knight of Northumberland,
502Have for the gilt of France — oh, guilt indeed! —
503Confirmed conspiracy with fearful France.
504And by their hands this grace of kings must die,
505If hell and treason hold their promises,
506Ere he take ship for France, and in Southampton.
507Linger your patience on, and we'll digest
508Th'abuse of distance, force a play.
509The sum is paid, the traitors are agreed,
510The king is set from London, and the scene
511Is now transported, gentles, to Southampton.
512There is the playhouse now, there must you sit,
513And thence to France shall we convey you safe
514And bring you back, charming the narrow seas
515To give you gentle pass; for if we may,
516We'll not offend one stomach with our play.
517But when the king come forth, and not till then,
518Unto Southampton do we shift our scene.

Exit

[2.1]

Enter Corporal Nym and Lieutenant Bardolph.

BARDOLPH

Well met, Corporal Nym.

NYM

Good morrow, Lieutenant Bardolph.

BARDOLPH

What, are Ancient Pistol and you friends yet?

NYM

For my part, I care not. I say little, but when time shall serve, there shall be smiles. But that shall be as it may. I dare not fight, but I will wink and hold out mine iron. It is a simple one, but what though? It will toast cheese, and it will endure cold as another man's sword will, and there's an end.

BARDOLPH

I will bestow a breakfast to make you friends, and we'll be all three sworn brothers to France. Let’t be so, good Corporal Nym.

NYM

Faith, I will live so long as I may, that's the certain of it. And when I cannot live any longer, I will do as I may. That is my rest; that is the rendezvous of it.

BARDOLPH

It is certain, corporal, that he is married to Nell Quickly, and certainly she did you wrong, for you were troth-plight to her.

NYM

I cannot tell; things must be as they may. Men may sleep, and they may have their throats about them at that time, and some say knives have edges. It must be as it may. Though patience be a tired mare, yet she will plod. There must be conclusions. Well, I cannot tell.

Enter Pistol and [Hostess, formerly Mistress] Quickly.

BARDOLPH

Here comes Ancient Pistol and his wife. Good corporal, be patient here. — How now, mine host Pistol?

PISTOL

Base tyke, call'st thou me host? Now by this hand I swear I scorn the term! Nor shall my Nell keep lodgers.

HOSTESS

No, by my troth, not long, for we cannot lodge and board a dozen or fourteen gentlewomen that live honestly by the prick of their needles, but it will be thought we keep a bawdy house straight. [Nym draws his sword.] Oh, welladay, lady, if he be not hewn now, we shall see willful adultery and murder committed!

BARDOLPH

Good lieutenant, good corporal, offer nothing here.

NYM

Pish.

PISTOL

Pish for thee, Iceland dog, thou prick-eared cur of Iceland.

HOSTESS

Good Corporal Nym, show thy valor and put up your sword.

NYM

Will you shog off? [To Pistol] I would have you solus.

PISTOL

"Solus," egregious dog? O viper vile! The "solus" in thy most marvelous face. The "solus" in thy teeth, and in thy throat, and in thy hateful lungs, yea in thy maw, perdy. And which is worse, within thy nasty mouth. I do retort the "solus" in thy bowels, for I can take, and Pistol's cock is up, and flashing fire will follow.

NYM

I am not Barbason; you cannot conjure me. I have an humor to knock you indifferently well. If you grow foul with me, Pistol, I will scour you with my rapier, as I may, in fair terms. If you would walk off, I would prick your guts a little in good terms, as I may, and that's the humor of it.

PISTOL

519O braggart vile, and damnèd furious wight,
520The grave doth gape, and doting death is near,
521Therefore exhale.

[Pistol draws his sword.]

BARDOLPH

Hear me, hear me what I say. [Draws his sword (?)] He that strikes the first stroke, I'll run him up to the hilts, as I am a soldier.

PISTOL

An oath of mickle might, and fury shall abate. [They sheathe their swords.] Give me thy fist. Thy forefoot to me give. Thy spirits are most tall.

NYM

I will cut thy throat one time or other in fair terms. That is the humor of it.

PISTOL

Couple a gorge, that is the word. I defy thee again! O hound of Crete, think'st thou my spouse to get? No, to the Spital go, and from the powd'ring tub of infamy fetch forth the lazar kite of Cressid's kind, Doll Tearsheet, she by name, and her espouse. I have, and I will hold the quondam Quickly for the only she, and pauca, there's enough. Go to.

Enter the Boy.

BOY

Mine host Pistol, you must come to my master, and your hostess. He is very sick and would to bed. — Good Bardolph, put thy face between his sheets and do the office of a warming-pan. Faith, he's very ill.

BARDOLPH

Away, you rogue.

HOSTESS

By my troth, he'll yield the crow a pudding one of these days. The king has killed his heart. Good husband, come home presently.

Exeunt [Boy and Hostess.]

BARDOLPH

Come, shall I make you two friends? We must to France together. Why the devil should we keep knives to cut one another's throats?

PISTOL

Let floods o'erswell, and fiends for food howl on.

NYM

[To Pistol] You'll pay me the eight shillings I won of you at betting?

PISTOL

Base is the slave that pays.

NYM

That now I will have. That's the humor of it.

PISTOL

As manhood shall compound. Push home.

[They] draw [their swords.]

BARDOLPH

[Drawing his sword (?)] By this sword, he that makes the first thrust, I'll kill him. By this sword, I will.

PISTOL

Sword is an oath, and oaths must have their course.

[Sheathes his sword]

BARDOLPH

Corporal Nym, an thou wilt be friends, be friends. An thou wilt not, why then be enemies with me, too. Prithee, put up.

[Nym and Bardolph sheathe their swords. (?)]

PISTOL

A noble shalt thou have, and present pay. And liquor likewise will I give to thee, and friendship shall combine, and brotherhood. I'll live by Nym and Nym shall live by me. Is not this just? For I shall sutler be unto the camp, and profits will accrue. Give me thy hand.

NYM

I shall have my noble?

PISTOL

In cash most justly paid.

NYM

Well, then that's the humor of't.

[Pistol and Nym shake hands. (?)]

Enter Hostess.

HOSTESS

As ever you come of women, come in quickly to Sir John. A poor heart, he is so shaked of a burning quotidian tertian that it is most lamentable to behold. Sweet men, come to him.

[Exit.]

NYM

The king hath run bad humors on the knight. That's the even of it.

PISTOL

Nym, thou hast spoke the right. His heart is fracted and corroborate.

NYM

The king is a good king, but it must be as it may. He passes some humors and careers.

PISTOL

Let us condole the knight, for lambkins, we will live.

[Exeunt.]

[2.2]

Enter Exeter, Bedford, and Westmorland.

BEDFORD

522'Fore God, his grace is bold to trust these traitors.

EXETER

523They shall be apprehended by and by.

WESTMORLAND

524How smooth and even they do bear themselves,
525As if allegiance in their bosoms sat
526Crownèd with faith and constant loyalty.

BEDFORD

527The king hath note of all that they intend
528By interception, which they dream not of.

EXETER

529Nay, but the man that was his bedfellow,
530Whom he hath dulled and cloyed with gracious favors,
531That he should for a foreign purse so sell
532His sovereign's life to death and treachery!

Sound trumpets. Enter the King, Scrope, Cambridge, and Grey[, and attendants.]

KING HENRY

533Now sits the wind fair, and we will aboard. —
534My lord of Cambridge, and my kind lord of Masham,
535And you, my gentle knight, give me your thoughts:
536Think you not that the powers we bear with us
537Will cut their passage through the force of France,
538Doing the execution and the act
539For which we have in head assembled them?

SCROPE

540No doubt, my liege, if each man do his best.

KING HENRY

541I doubt not that, since we are well persuaded
542We carry not a heart with us from hence
543That grows not in a fair consent with ours,
544Nor leave not one behind that doth not wish
545Success and conquest to attend on us.

CAMBRIDGE

546Never was monarch better feared and loved
547Than is your majesty. There's not, I think, a subject
548That sits in heart-grief and uneasiness
549Under the sweet shade of your government.

GREY

550True. Those that were your father's enemies
551Have steeped their galls in honey and do serve you
552With hearts create of duty and of zeal.

KING HENRY

553We therefore have great cause of thankfulness,
554And shall forget the office of our hand
555Sooner than quittance of desert and merit,
556According to the weight and worthiness.

SCROPE

557So service shall with steelèd sinews toil,
558And labor shall refresh itself with hope
559To do your grace incessant services.

KING HENRY

560We judge no less. Uncle of Exeter,
561Enlarge the man committed yesterday
562That railed against our person. We consider
563It was excess of wine that set him on,
564And on his more advice we pardon him.

SCROPE

565That's mercy, but too much security.
566Let him be punished, sovereign, lest example
567Breed, by his sufferance, more of such a kind.

KING HENRY

568Oh, let us yet be merciful.

CAMBRIDGE

569So may your highness, and yet punish too.

GREY

570Sir, you show great mercy if you give him life
571After the taste of much correction.

KING HENRY

572Alas, your too much love and care of me
573Are heavy orisons 'gainst this poor wretch.
574If little faults proceeding on distemper
575Shall not be winked at, how shall we stretch our eye
576When capital crimes, chewed, swallowed, and digested,
577Appear before us? We'll yet enlarge that man,
578Though Cambridge, Scrope, and Grey, in their dear care
579And tender preservation of our person
580Would have him punished. And now to our French causes. —
581Who are the late commissioners?

CAMBRIDGE

Who are the late commissioners? I one, my lord.
582Your highness bade me ask for it today.

SCROPE

583So did you me, my liege.

GREY

584And I, my royal sovereign.

KING HENRY

585[Giving them papers] Then Richard Earl of Cambridge, there is yours.
586There yours, Lord Scrope of Masham; and sir knight,
587Grey of Northumberland, this same is yours.
588Read them and know I know your worthiness. —
589My lord of Westmorland, and uncle Exeter,
590We will aboard to night. — Why, how now, gentlemen?
591What see you in those papers that you lose
592So much complexion? Look ye how they change:
593Their cheeks are paper! Why, what read you there
594That have so cowarded and chased your blood
595Out of appearance?

CAMBRIDGE

Out of appearance? I do confess my fault,
596And do submit me to your highness' mercy.

GREY, SCROPE

597To which we all appeal.

KING HENRY

598The mercy that was quick in us but late
599By your own counsel is suppressed and killed.
600You must not dare for shame to talk of mercy,
601For your own reasons turn into your bosoms
602As dogs upon their masters, worrying you. —
603See you, my princes and my noble peers,
604These English monsters: my lord of Cambridge here,
605You know how apt our love was to accord
606To furnish him with all appurtenants
607Belonging to his honor. And this man
608Hath for a few light crowns lightly conspired
609And sworn unto the practices of France
610To kill us here in Hampton. To the which
611This knight, no less for bounty bound to us
612Than Cambridge is, hath likewise sworn.— But oh,
613What shall I say to thee, Lord Scrope, thou cruel,
614Ingrateful, savage and inhuman creature?
615Thou that didst bear the key of all my counsels,
616That knew'st the very bottom of my soul,
617That almost mightst have coined me into gold,
618Wouldst thou have practiced on me for thy use?
619May it be possible that foreign hire
620Could out of thee extract one spark of evil
621That might annoy my finger? 'Tis so strange
622That though the truth of it stands off as gross
623As black and white, my eye will scarcely see it.
624Treason and murder ever kept together
625As two yoke-devils sworn to either's purpose,
626Working so grossly in unnatural cause
627That admiration did not whoop at them.
628But thou, 'gainst all proportion, didst bring in
629Wonder to wait on treason and on murder,
630And whatsoever cunning fiend it was
631That wrought upon thee so preposterously
632Hath got the voice in hell for excellence;
633And other devils, that suggest by treasons,
634Do botch and bungle up damnation
635With patches, colors, and with forms being fetched
636From glist'ring semblances of piety.
637But he that tempered thee, bade thee stand up,
638Gave thee no instance why thou shouldst do treason
639Unless to dub thee with the name of traitor.
640If that same demon that hath gulled thee thus
641Should with his lion gait walk the whole world,
642He might return to vasty Tartar back
643And tell the legions, "I can never win
644A soul so easy as that Englishman's."
645Oh, how hast thou with jealousy infected
646The sweetness of affiance! Show men dutiful?
647Why so didst thou. Seem they grave and learnèd?
648Why so didst thou. Come they of noble family?
649Why so didst thou. Seem they religious?
650Why so didst thou. Or are they spare in diet,
651Free from gross passion or of mirth or anger,
652Constant in spirit, not swerving with the blood,
653Garnished and decked in modest complement,
654Not working with the eye without the ear,
655And but in purgèd judgment trusting neither?
656Such and so finely bolted didst thou seem.
657And thus thy fall hath left a kind of blot
658To make the full-fraught man, and best, indued
659With some suspicion. I will weep for thee,
660For this revolt of thine, methinks, is like
661Another fall of man.— Their faults are open.
662Arrest them to the answer of the law,
663And God acquit them of their practices.

EXETER

I arrest thee of high treason, by the name of Richard Earl of Cambridge. I arrest thee of high treason, by the name of Henry Lord Scrope of Masham. I arrest thee of high treason, by the name of Thomas Grey, knight of Northumberland.

SCROPE

664Our purposes God justly hath discovered,
665And I repent my fault more than my death,
666Which I beseech your highness to forgive,
667Although my body pay the price of it.

CAMBRIDGE

668For me, the gold of France did not seduce,
669Although I did admit it as a motive
670The sooner to effect what I intended.
671But God be thankèd for prevention,
672Which I in sufferance heartily will rejoice,
673Beseeching God and you to pardon me.

GREY

674Never did faithful subject more rejoice
675At the discovery of most dangerous treason
676Than I do at this hour joy o'er myself,
677Prevented from a damnèd enterprise.
678My fault, but not my body, pardon, sovereign.

KING HENRY

679God quit you in his mercy. Hear your sentence:
680You have conspired against our royal person,
681Joined with an enemy proclaimed and from his coffers
682Received the golden earnest of our death,
683Wherein you would have sold your king to slaughter,
684His princes and his peers to servitude,
685His subjects to oppression and contempt,
686And his whole kingdom into desolation.
687Touching our person seek we no revenge,
688But we our kingdom's safety must so tender,
689Whose ruin you sought, that to her laws
690We do deliver you. Get you therefore hence,
691Poor miserable wretches, to your death,
692The taste whereof God of his mercy give
693You patience to endure, and true repentance
694Of all your dear offences. — Bear them hence.

[Exeunt traitors, guarded.]

695Now, lords, for France, the enterprise whereof
696Shall be to you as us like glorious.
697We doubt not of a fair and lucky war,
698Since God so graciously hath brought to light
699This dangerous treason lurking in our way
700To hinder our beginnings. We doubt not now
701But every rub is smoothèd on our way.
702Then forth, dear countrymen. Let us deliver
703Our puissance into the hand of God,
704Putting it straight in expedition.
705Cheerly to sea; the signs of war advance.
706No king of England if not king of France.

Flourish. [Exeunt.]

[2.3]

Enter Pistol, Nym, Bardolph, Boy, and Hostess.

HOSTESS

Prithee, honey-sweet husband, let me bring thee to Staines.

PISTOL

No, for my manly heart doth earn. Bardolph, be blithe. Nym, rouse thy vaunting veins. Boy, bristle thy courage up. For Falstaff he is dead, and we must earn therefore.

BARDOLPH

Would I were with him wheresome'er he is, either in heaven or in hell.

HOSTESS

Nay, sure he's not in hell. He's in Arthur's bosom, if ever man went to Arthur's bosom. A made a finer end, and went away an it had been any christom child. A parted ev'n just between twelve and one, ev'n at the turning o'th'tide. For after I saw him fumble with the sheets, and play with flowers, and smile upon his finger's end, I knew there was but one way. For his nose was as sharp as a pen, and a babbled of green fields. "How now, Sir John?" quoth I. "What, man, be o' good cheer!" So a cried out, "God, God, God," three or four times. Now I, to comfort him, bid him a should not think of God; I hoped there was no need to trouble himself with any such thoughts yet. So a bade me lay more clothes on his feet. I put my hand into the bed and felt them, and they were as cold as any stone. Then I felt to his knees, and so up-peered, and upward and all was as cold as any stone.

NYM

They say he cried out of sack.

HOSTESS

Ay, that a did.

BARDOLPH

And of women.

HOSTESS

Nay, that a did not.

BOY

Yes, that a did, and said they were devils incarnate.

HOSTESS

A could never abide carnation; 'twas a color he never liked.

BOY

A said once the devil would have him about women.

HOSTESS

A did in some sort, indeed, handle women, but then he was rheumatic, and talked of the whore of Babylon.

BOY

Do you not remember a saw a flea stick upon Bardolph's nose, and a said it was a black soul burning in hell?

BARDOLPH

Well, the fuel is gone that maintained that fire. That's all the riches I got in his service.

NYM

Shall we shog? The king will be gone from Southampton.

PISTOL

Come, let's away. My love, give me thy lips. [Kisses her] Look to my chattels and my moveables. Let senses rule. The world is pitch-and-pay. Trust none, for oaths are straws, men's faiths are wafer-cakes, and Holdfast is the only dog, my duck, therefore caveto be thy counselor. Go, clear thy crystals. Yoke-fellows in arms, let us to France like horse-leeches, my boys: to suck, to suck, the very blood to suck!

BOY

And that's but unwholesome food, they say.

PISTOL

Touch her soft mouth and march.

BARDOLPH

[Kisses her] Farewell, hostess.

NYM

I cannot kiss, that is the humor of it, but adieu.

PISTOL

Let housewifery appear. Keep close, I thee command.

HOSTESS

Farewell. Adieu.

Exeunt.

[2.4]

Flourish. Enter the French King, the Dauphin, the Dukes of Berry and Brittany[, and the Constable of France].

FRENCH KING

707Thus comes the English with full power upon us,
708And more than carefully it us concerns
709To answer royally in our defenses.
710Therefore the Dukes of Berry and of Brittany,
711Of Brabant and of Orléans shall make forth,
712And you, Prince Dauphin, with all swift dispatch
713To line and new repair our towns of war
714With men of courage and with means defendant,
715For England his approaches makes as fierce
716As waters to the sucking of a gulf.
717It fits us then to be as provident
718As fear may teach us, out of late examples
719Left by the fatal and neglected English
720Upon our fields.

DAUPHIN

Upon our fields. My most redoubted father,
721It is most meet we arm us 'gainst the foe,
722For peace itself should not so dull a kingdom,
723Though war nor no known quarrel were in question,
724But that defenses, musters, preparations
725Should be maintained, assembled, and collected
726As were a war in expectation.
727Therefore I say 'tis meet we all go forth
728To view the sick and feeble parts of France.
729And let us do it with no show of fear,
730No, with no more than if we heard that England
731Were busied with a Whitsun morris dance.
732For, my good liege, she is so idly kinged,
733Her scepter so fantastically borne
734By a vain, giddy, shallow, humorous youth,
735That fear attends her not.

CONSTABLE

That fear attends her not. Oh, peace, Prince Dauphin.
736You are too much mistaken in this king.
737Question your grace the late ambassadors —
738With what great state he heard their embassy,
739How well supplied with noble counselors,
740How modest in exception, and withal
741How terrible in constant resolution —
742And you shall find his vanities forespent
743Were but the outside of the Roman Brutus,
744Covering discretion with a coat of folly,
745As gardeners do with ordure hide those roots
746That shall first spring and be most delicate.

DAUPHIN

747Well, 'tis not so, my lord high constable.
748But though we think it so, it is no matter.
749In cases of defense, 'tis best to weigh
750The enemy more mighty than he seems.
751So the proportions of defense are filled,
752Which of a weak and niggardly projection
753Doth like a miser spoil his coat with scanting
754A little cloth.

FRENCH KING

A little cloth. Think we King Harry strong,
755And princes, look you strongly arm to meet him.
756The kindred of him hath been fleshed upon us,
757And he is bred out of that bloody strain
758That haunted us in our familiar paths:
759Witness our too much memorable shame
760When Crécy battle fatally was struck,
761And all our princes captived by the hand
762Of that black name, Edward, Black Prince of Wales,
763Whiles that his mountain sire on mountain standing
764Up in the air, crowned with the golden sun,
765Saw his heroical seed and smiled to see him
766Mangle the work of nature, and deface
767The patterns that by God and by French fathers
768Had twenty years been made. This is a stem
769Of that victorious stock, and let us fear
770The native mightiness and fate of him.

Enter a Messenger.

MESSENGER

771Ambassadors from Harry, King of England,
772Do crave admittance to your majesty.

FRENCH KING

773We'll give them present audience; go and bring them.

[Exit Messenger.]

774You see this chase is hotly followed, friends.

DAUPHIN

775Turn head and stop pursuit, for coward dogs
776Most spend their mouths when what they seem to threaten
777Runs far before them. Good my sovereign,
778Take up the English short, and let them know
779Of what a monarchy you are the head.
780Self-love, my liege, is not so vile a sin
781As self-neglecting.

Enter Exeter.

FRENCH KING

As self-neglecting. From our brother of England?

EXETER

782From him, and thus he greets your majesty:
783He wills you in the name of God almighty
784That you divest yourself, and lay apart
785The borrowed glories that by gift of heaven,
786By law of nature and of nations longs
787To him and to his heirs, namely the crown
788And all wide-stretchèd honors that pertain
789By custom and the ordinance of times
790Unto the crown of France. That you may know
791'Tis no sinister nor no awkward claim
792Picked from the wormholes of long-vanished days,
793Nor from the dust of old oblivion raked,
794He sends you this most memorable line,

[Gives the French King a paper]

795In every branch truly demonstrative,
796Willing you overlook this pedigree.
797And when you find him evenly derived
798From his most famed of famous ancestors,
799Edward the Third, he bids you then resign
800Your crown and kingdom, indirectly held
801From him, the native and true challenger.

FRENCH KING

802Or else what follows?

EXETER

803Bloody constraint: for if you hide the crown
804Even in your hearts, there will he rake for it.
805Therefore in fierce tempest is he coming,
806In thunder and in earthquake, like a Jove,
807That if requiring fail, he will compel.
808And bids you in the bowels of the Lord
809Deliver up the crown, and to take mercy
810On the poor souls for whom this hungry war
811Opens his vasty jaws, and on your head
812Turning the widows' tears, the orphans' cries,
813The dead men's blood, the privy maidens' groans
814For husbands, fathers, and betrothèd lovers
815That shall be swallowed in this controversy.
816This is his claim, his threat'ning, and my message,
817Unless the dauphin be in presence here,
818To whom expressly I bring greeting too.

FRENCH KING

819For us, we will consider of this further.
820Tomorrow shall you bear our full intent
821Back to our brother of England.

DAUPHIN

Back to our brother of England. For the dauphin,
822I stand here for him. What to him from England?

EXETER

823Scorn and defiance, slight regard, contempt,
824And anything that may not misbecome
825The mighty sender doth he prize you at.
826Thus says my king: an if your father's highness
827Do not, in grant of all demands at large,
828Sweeten the bitter mock you sent his majesty,
829He'll call you to so hot an answer of it
830That caves and womby vaultages of France
831Shall chide your trespass and return your mock
832In second accent of his ordinance.

DAUPHIN

833Say if my father render fair return
834It is against my will, for I desire
835Nothing but odds with England. To that end,
836As matching to his youth and vanity,
837I did present him with the Paris balls.

EXETER

838He'll make your Paris Louvre shake for it,
839Were it the mistress court of mighty Europe.
840And be assured, you'll find a difference,
841As we his subjects have in wonder found,
842Between the promise of his greener days
843And these he masters now. Now he weighs time
844Even to the utmost grain. That you shall read
845In your own losses, if he stay in France.

FRENCH KING

846Tomorrow shall you know our mind at full.

Flourish.

EXETER

847Dispatch us with all speed, lest that our king
848Come here himself to question our delay,
849For he is footed in this land already.

FRENCH KING

850You shall be soon dispatched with fair conditions.
851A night is but small breath and little pause
852To answer matters of this consequence.

Exeunt.

Act III

[3.0]

Flourish. Enter Chorus.

CHORUS

853Thus with imagined wing our swift scene flies
854In motion of no less celerity
855Than that of thought. Suppose that you have seen
856The well-appointed king at Dover pier
857Embark his royalty, and his brave fleet
858With silken streamers the young Phoebus feigning.
859Play with your fancies, and in them behold
860Upon the hempen tackle ship-boys climbing.
861Hear the shrill whistle, which doth order give
862To sounds confused. Behold the threaden sails,
863Borne with th'invisible and creeping wind,
864Draw the huge bottoms through the furrowed sea,
865Breasting the lofty surge. Oh, do but think
866You stand upon the rivage, and behold
867A city on th'inconstant billows dancing,
868For so appears this fleet majestical
869Holding due course to Harfleur. Follow, follow!
870Grapple your minds to sternage of this navy
871And leave your England as dead midnight, still,
872Guarded with grandsires, babies, and old women,
873Either past or not arrived to pith and puissance.
874For who is he whose chin is but enriched
875With one appearing hair, that will not follow
876These culled and choice-drawn cavaliers to France?
877Work, work your thoughts, and therein see a siege.
878Behold the ordnance on their carriages,
879With fatal mouths gaping on girded Harfleur.
880Suppose th'ambassador from the French comes back,
881Tells Harry that the king doth offer him
882Catherine his daughter, and with her to dowry
883Some petty and unprofitable dukedoms.
884The offer likes not, and the nimble gunner
885With linstock now the devilish cannon touches,

Alarum, and chambers go off.

886And down goes all before them. Still be kind,
887And eke out our performance with your mind.

Exit.

[3.1]

Enter the King, Exeter, Bedford, and Gloucester.

Alarum. [Enter soldiers with] scaling-ladders at Harfleur.

KING HENRY

888Once more unto the breach, dear friends, once more,
889Or close the wall up with our English dead!
890In peace, there's nothing so becomes a man
891As modest stillness and humility,
892But when the blast of war blows in our ears,
893Then imitate the action of the tiger:
894Stiffen the sinews, conjure up the blood,
895Disguise fair nature with hard-favored rage.
896Then lend the eye a terrible aspect;
897Let it pry through the portage of the head
898Like the brass cannon. Let the brow o'erwhelm it
899As fearfully as doth a gallèd rock
900O'erhang and jutty his confounded base,
901Swilled with the wild and wasteful ocean.
902Now set the teeth and stretch the nostril wide,
903Hold hard the breath and bend up every spirit
904To his full height. On! On, you noble English,
905Whose blood is fet from fathers of war-proof,
906Fathers that, like so many Alexanders,
907Have in these parts from morn till even fought
908And sheathed their swords for lack of argument.
909Dishonor not your mothers; now attest
910That those whom you called fathers did beget you.
911Be copy now to men of grosser blood
912And teach them how to war. And you good yeomen,
913Whose limbs were made in England, show us here
914The mettle of your pasture. Let us swear
915That you are worth your breeding, which I doubt not,
916For there is none of you so mean and base
917That hath not noble luster in your eyes.
918I see you stand like greyhounds in the slips,
919Straining upon the start. The game's afoot:
920Follow your spirit, and upon this charge
921Cry "God for Harry, England, and Saint George!"

Alarum, and chambers go off. [Exeunt.]

[3.2]

Enter Nym, Bardolph, Pistol, and Boy.

BARDOLPH

On, on, on, on, on, to the breach, to the breach!

NYM

Pray thee, corporal, stay. The knocks are too hot, and for mine own part, I have not a case of lives. The humor of it is too hot, that is the very plainsong of it.

PISTOL

The plainsong is most just, for humors do abound, knocks go and come, God's vassals drop and die,

[Singing]

922And sword and shield
923In bloody field
924Doth win immortal fame.

BOY

Would I were in an alehouse in London. I would give all my fame for a pot of ale, and safety.

PISTOL

And I.

925[Singing] If wishes would prevail with me,
926My purpose should not fail with me,
927But thither would I hie.

BOY

928[Singing] As duly —
929But not as truly —
930As bird doth sing on bough.

Enter Fluellen.

FLUELLEN

[Beating them] Up to the breach, you dogs! Avaunt, you cullions!

PISTOL

Be merciful, great duke, to men of mold! Abate thy rage, abate thy manly rage! Abate thy rage, great duke! Good bawcock, bate thy rage. Use lenity, sweet chuck.

NYM

These be good humors! Your honor wins bad humors!

[Exeunt Pistol, Bardolph, and Nym.]

BOY

[To audience] As young as I am, I have observed these three swashers. I am boy to them all three, but all they three, though they would serve me, could not be man to me, for indeed three such antics do not amount to a man. For Bardolph, he is white-livered and red-faced, by the means whereof a faces it out, but fights not. For Pistol, he hath a killing tongue and a quiet sword, by the means whereof a breaks words and keeps whole weapons. For Nym, he hath heard that men of few words are the best men, and therefore he scorns to say his prayers, lest a should be thought a coward. But his few bad words are matched with as few good deeds, for a never broke any man's head but his own, and that was against a post when he was drunk. They will steal anything, and call it purchase. Bardolph stole a lute case, bore it twelve leagues, and sold it for three halfpence. Nym and Bardolph are sworn brothers in filching, and in Calais they stole a fire-shovel. I knew by that piece of service the men would carry coals. They would have me as familiar with men's pockets as their gloves or their handkercheifs, which makes much against my manhood, if I should take from another's pocket to put into mine, for it is plain pocketing up of wrongs. I must leave them and seek some better service. Their villainy goes against my weak stomach, and therefore I must cast it up.

Exit [Boy].

Enter Gower.

GOWER

Captain Fluellen, you must come presently to the mines; the Duke of Gloucester would speak with you.

FLUELLEN

To the mines? Tell you the duke it is not so good to come to the mines, for look you, the mines is not according to the disciplines of the war. The concavities of it is not sufficient: for look you, th'athversary, you may discuss unto the duke, look you, is digged himself, four yard under, the countermines. By Cheshu, I think a will plow up all if there is not better directions.

GOWER

The Duke of Gloucester, to whom the order of the siege is given, is altogether directed by an Irishman, a very valiant gentleman, i'faith.

FLUELLEN

It is Captain Macmorris, is it not?

GOWER

I think it be.

FLUELLEN

By Cheshu, he is an ass, as in the world. I will verify as much in his beard. He has no more directions in the true disciplines of the wars, look you, of the Roman disciplines, than is a puppydog.

Enter Macmorris and Captain Jamy.

GOWER

Here a comes, and the Scots captain, Captain Jamy, with him.

FLUELLEN

Captain Jamy is a marvelous falorous gentleman, that is certain, and of great expedition and knowledge in th'aunchient wars, upon my particular knowledge of his directions. By Cheshu, he will maintain his argument as well as any military man in the world, in the disciplines of the pristine wars of the Romans.

JAMY

I say guid day, Captain Fluellen.

FLUELLEN

Good e'en to your worship, good Captain James.

GOWER

How now, Captain Macmorris, have you quit the mines? Have the pioneers given o'er?

MACMORRIS

By Chrish law, 'tish ill done. The work ish give over, the trumpet sound the retreat. By my hand I swear, and my father's soul, the work ish ill done; it ish give over. I would have blowed up the town, so Chrish save me law, in an hour. Oh, 'tish ill done, 'tish ill done, by my hand 'tish ill done.

FLUELLEN

Captain Macmorris, I beseech you now, will you vouchsafe me, look you, a few disputations with you, as partly touching or concerning the disciplines of the war, the Roman wars, in the way of argument, look you, and friendly communication? Partly to satisfy my opinion, and partly for the satisfaction, look you, of my mind, as touching the direction of the military discipline; that is the point.

JAMY

It sall be verray guid, guid faith, guid captains baith, and I sall quit you with guid leve, as I may pick occasion. That sall I, marry.

MACMORRIS

It is no time to discourse, so Chrish save me. The day is hot, and the weather, and the wars, and the king, and the dukes. It is no time to discourse. The town is besieched, and the trumpet call us to the breach, and we talk, and be Chrish do nothing! 'Tis shame for us all; so God sa' me, 'tis shame to stand still. It is shame, by my hand; and there is throats to be cut, and works to be done, and there ish nothing done, so Christ sa' me law.

JAMY

By the mess, ere these eyes of mine take themselves to slumber, I'll dae guid service, or I'll lig i'th' grund for it; I owe God a death, and I'll pay't as valorously as I may, that sall I surely do. That is the brefe and the long. Marry, I wad full fain heard some question 'tween you twae.

FLUELLEN

Captain Macmorris, I think, look you, under your correction, there is not many of your nation —

MACMORRIS

Of my nation? What ish my nation? Ish a villain, and a bastard, and a knave, and a rascal? What ish my nation? Who talks of my nation?

FLUELLEN

Look you, if you take the matter otherwise than is meant, Captain Macmorris, peradventure I shall think you do not use me with that affability as in discretion you ought to use me, look you, being as good a man as yourself, both in the disciplines of war and in the derivation of my birth, and in other particularities.

MACMORRIS

I do not know you so good a man as myself. So Chrish save me, I will cut off your head.

GOWER

Gentlemen both, you will mistake each other.

JAMY

Ah, that's a foul fault.

A parley [is sounded.]

GOWER

The town sounds a parley.

FLUELLEN

Captain Macmorris, when there is more better opportunity to be required, look you, I will be so bold as to tell you I know the disciplines of war, and there is an end.

[Exeunt.]

[3.3]

Enter the King and all his train before the gates.

KING HENRY

931How yet resolves the governor of the town?
932This is the latest parle we will admit,
933Therefore to our best mercy give yourselves,
934Or like to men proud of destruction
935Defy us to our worst; for as I am a soldier,
936A name that in my thoughts becomes me best,
937If I begin the batt'ry once again,
938I will not leave the half-achieved Harfleur
939Till in her ashes she lie burièd.
940The gates of mercy shall be all shut up,
941And the fleshed soldier, rough and hard of heart,
942In liberty of bloody hand shall range
943With conscience wide as hell, mowing like grass
944Your fresh fair virgins and your flow'ring infants.
945What is it then to me if impious war,
946Arrayed in flames like to the prince of fiends,
947Do with his smirched complexion all fell feats
948Enlinked to waste and desolation?
949What is't to me, when you yourselves are cause,
950If your pure maidens fall into the hand
951Of hot and forcing violation?
952What rein can hold licentious wickedness
953When down the hill he holds his fierce career?
954We may as bootless spend our vain command
955Upon th'enragèd soldiers in their spoil
956As send precepts to the leviathan
957To come ashore. Therefore, you men of Harfleur,
958Take pity of your town and of your people
959Whiles yet my soldiers are in my command,
960Whiles yet the cool and temperate wind of grace
961O'er-blows the filthy and contagious clouds
962Of headly murder, spoil, and villainy.
963If not, why in a moment look to see
964The blind and bloody soldier with foul hand
965Defile the locks of your shrill-shrieking daughters,
966Your fathers taken by the silver beards
967And their most reverend heads dashed to the walls,
968Your naked infants spitted upon pikes
969Whiles the mad mothers with their howls confused
970Do break the clouds, as did the wives of Jewry
971At Herod's bloody-hunting slaughtermen.
972What say you? Will you yield and this avoid,
973Or, guilty in defense, be thus destroyed?

Enter Governor.

GOVERNOR

974Our expectation hath this day an end:
975The dauphin, whom of succors we entreated,
976Returns us that his powers are yet not ready
977To raise so great a siege. Therefore, great king,
978We yield our town and lives to thy soft mercy.
979Enter our gates; dispose of us and ours,
980For we no longer are defensible.

KING HENRY

981Open your gates. –

[Exit Governor.]

Open your gates. – Come, uncle Exeter,
982Go you and enter Harfleur; there remain
983And fortify it strongly 'gainst the French.
984Use mercy to them all for us, dear uncle.
985The winter coming on and sickness growing
986Upon our soldiers, we will retire to Calais.
987Tonight in Harfleur will we be your guest;
988Tomorrow for the march are we addressed.

Flourish, and [the English] enter the town.

[3.4]

Enter Catherine and [Alice,] an old gentlewoman.

CATHERINE

Alice, tu as été en Angleterre, et tu bien parles le langage.

ALICE

Un peu, madame.

CATHERINE

Je te prie, m'enseignez; il faut que j'apprenne à parler. Comment appelez-vous la main en anglais?

ALICE

La main? Elle est appellée de hand.

CATHERINE

De hand. Et les doigts ?

ALICE

Les doigts — ma foi, j'oublie les doigts! Mais je me souviendrai: les doigts, je pense qu'ils sont appellés de fingres. Oui, de fingres.

CATHERINE

Le main, de hand, les doigts, les fingres. Je pense que je suis la bonne écolière. J'ai gagné deux mots d'anglais vistement. Comment appelez vous les ongles?

ALICE

Les ongles, nous les appelons de nails.

CATHERINE

De nails. Écoutez; dites-moi si je parle bien: de hand, de fingres, et de nails.

ALICE

C'est bien dit, madame. Il est fort bon anglais.

CATHERINE

Dites-moi l'anglais pour le bras.

ALICE

De arm, madame.

CATHERINE

Et le coude?

ALICE

D'elbow.

CATHERINE

D'elbow. Je m'en fais la répétition de tous les mots que vous m'avez appris dès à présent.

ALICE

Il est trop difficile, madame, comme je pense.

CATHERINE

Excusez-moi, Alice. Éscoutez: d'hand, de fingre, de nails, d'arma, de bilbow.

ALICE

D'elbow, madame.

CATHERINE

O Seigneur Dieu, je m'en oublie! D'elbow. Comment appellez-vous le col ?

ALICE

De nick, madame.

CATHERINE

De nick. Et le menton?

ALICE

De chin.

CATHERINE

De sin. Le col, de nick, le menton, de sin.

ALICE

Oui. Sauf votre honneur, en vérité vous prononcez les mots aussi droit que les natifs d’Angleterre.

CATHERINE

Je ne doute point d'apprendre, par la grâce de Dieu, et en peu de temps.

ALICE

N'avez-vous déjà oublié ce que je vous ai enseigné ?

CATHERINE

Non, et je réciterai à vous promptement: d'hand, de fingre, de mailés —

ALICE

De nails, madame.

CATHERINE

De nails, de arm, de ilbow —

ALICE

Sauf votre honneur, d'elbow.

CATHERINE

Ainsi dis-je, d'elbow. De nick, et de sin. Comment appellez-vous le pied et la robe ?

ALICE

Le foot, madame, et le count.

CATHERINE

Le foot et le count? O Seigneur Dieu, ils sont les mots de son mauvais, corruptible, gros, et impudique, et non pour les dames d'honneur d'user! Je ne voudrais prononcer ces mots devant les seigneurs de France pour tout le monde. Foh! Le foot et le count! Néanmoins, je réciterai une autre fois ma leçon ensemble: d'hand, de fingre, de nails, d'arm, d'elbow, de nick, de sin, de foot, le count.

ALICE

Excellent, madame!

CATHERINE

C'est assez pour une fois. Allons-nous à dîner.

[Exeunt.]

[3.5]

Enter the King of France, the Dauphin, the Constable of France, [the Duke of Brittany,] and others.

FRENCH KING

989'Tis certain he hath passed the river Somme.

CONSTABLE

990And if he be not fought withal, my lord,
991Let us not live in France. Let us quit all
992And give our vineyards to a barbarous people.

DAUPHIN

993O Dieu vivant! Shall a few sprays of us,
994The emptying of our fathers' luxury,
995Our scions, put in wild and savage stock,
996Spurt up so suddenly into the clouds
997And overlook their grafters?

BRITTANY

998Normans, but bastard Normans! Norman bastards!
999Mort de ma vie, if they march along
1000Unfought withal, but I will sell my dukedom
1001To buy a slobb'ry and a dirty farm
1002In that nook-shotten isle of Albion.

CONSTABLE

1003Dieu des batailles, where have they this mettle?
1004Is not their climate foggy, raw, and dull,
1005On whom, as in despite, the sun looks pale,
1006Killing their fruit with frowns? Can sodden water,
1007A drench for sur-reined jades, their barley broth,
1008Decoct their cold blood to such valiant heat?
1009And shall our quick blood, spirited with wine,
1010Seem frosty? Oh, for honor of our land,
1011Let us not hang like roping icicles
1012Upon our houses' thatch whiles a more frosty people
1013Sweat drops of gallant youth in our rich fields!
1014Poor we may call them in their native lords.

DAUPHIN

1015By faith and honor,
1016Our madams mock at us, and plainly say
1017Our mettle is bred out, and they will give
1018Their bodies to the lust of English youth
1019To new-store France with bastard warriors.

BRITTANY

1020They bid us to the English dancing schools
1021And teach lavoltas high and swift corantos,
1022Saying our grace is only in our heels
1023And that we are most lofty runaways.

FRENCH KING

1024Where is Montjoy the herald? Speed him hence.
1025Let him greet England with our sharp defiance.
1026Up, princes, and with spirit of honor edged
1027More sharper than your swords, hie to the field.
1028Charles d'Alberet, High Constable of France,
1029You Dukes of Orléans, Bourbon, and of Berry,
1030Alençon, Brabant, Bar, and Burgundy,
1031Jaques Châtillon, Rambures, Vaudémont,
1032Beaumont, Grandpré, Roucy, and Fauquembergues,
1033Foix, Lestrelles, Boucicaut, and Charolais,
1034High dukes, great princes, barons, lords, and kings,
1035For your great seats, now quit you of great shames.
1036Bar Harry England, that sweeps through our land
1037With pennons painted in the blood of Harfleur.
1038Rush on his host as doth the melted snow
1039Upon the valleys, whose low vassal seat
1040The Alps doth spit and void his rheum upon.
1041Go down upon him — you have power enough —
1042And in a captive chariot into Rouen
1043Bring him our prisoner.

CONSTABLE

Bring him our prisoner. This becomes the great.
1044Sorry am I his numbers are so few,
1045His soldiers sick and famished in their march;
1046For I am sure when he shall see our army
1047He'll drop his heart into the sink of fear
1048And, 'fore achievement, offer us his ransom.

FRENCH KING

1049Therefore, lord constable, haste on Montjoy
1050And let him say to England that we send
1051To know what willing ransom he will give.
1052Prince Dauphin, you shall stay with us in Rouen.

DAUPHIN

1053Not so, I do beseech your majesty.

FRENCH KING

1054Be patient, for you shall remain with us.
1055Now forth, lord constable, and princes all,
1056And quickly bring us word of England's fall.

Exeunt.

[3.6]

Enter Captains English and Welsh, Gower and Fluellen[, meeting].

GOWER

How now, Captain Fluellen, come you from the bridge?

FLUELLEN

I assure you, there is very excellent services committed at the bridge.

GOWER

Is the Duke of Exeter safe?

FLUELLEN

The Duke of Exeter is as magnanimous as Agamemnon, and a man that I love and honor with my soul, and my heart, and my duty, and my live, and my living, and my uttermost power. He is not, God be praised and blessed, any hurt in the world, but keeps the bridge most valiantly, with excellent discipline. There is an aunchient lieutenant there at the pridge. I think in my very conscience he is as valiant a man as Mark Antony, and he is a man of no estimation in the world, but I did see him do as gallant service.

GOWER

What do you call him?

FLUELLEN

He is called Aunchient Pistol.

GOWER

I know him not.

Enter Pistol.

FLUELLEN

Here is the man.

PISTOL

Captain, I thee beseech to do me favors; the Duke of Exeter doth love thee well.

FLUELLEN

Ay, I praise God, and I have merited some love at his hands.

PISTOL

Bardolph, a soldier firm and sound of heart, and of buxom valor, hath, by cruel fate and giddy Fortune's furious fickle wheel, that goddess blind that stands upon the rolling restless stone —

FLUELLEN

By your patience, Aunchient Pistol, Fortune is painted blind, with a muffler afore his eyes, to signify to you that fortune is blind; and she is painted also with a wheel, to signify to you, which is the moral of it, that she is turning and inconstant, and mutability, and variation; and her foot, look you, is fixed upon a spherical stone, which rowls and rowls and rowls. In good truth, the poet makes a most excellent description of it. Fortune is an excellent moral.

PISTOL

Fortune is Bardolph's foe and frowns on him, for he hath stolen a pax, and hanged must a be, a damned death. Let gallows gape for dog; let man go free, and let not hemp his windpipe suffocate. But Exeter hath given the doom of death for pax of little price. Therefore go speak — the duke will hear thy voice — and let not Bardolph's vital thread be cut with edge of penny-cord and vile reproach. Speak, captain, for his life, and I will thee requite.

FLUELLEN

Aunchient Pistol, I do partly understand your meaning.

PISTOL

Why then, rejoice therefore!

FLUELLEN

Certainly, aunchient, it is not a thing to rejoice at. For if, look you, he were my brother, I would desire the duke to use his good pleasure and put him to execution; for discipline ought to be used.

PISTOL

Die and be damned, and fico for thy friendship!

FLUELLEN

It is well.

PISTOL

The fig of Spain!

Exit.

FLUELLEN

Very good.

GOWER

Why, this is an arrant counterfeit rascal. I remember him now: a bawd, a cutpurse.

FLUELLEN

I'll assure you, a uttered as prave words at the pridge as you shall see in a summer's day. But it is very well; what he has spoke to me, that is well, I warrant you, when time is serve.

GOWER

Why 'tis a gull, a fool, a rogue that now and then goes to the wars to grace himself at his return into London under the form of a soldier. And such fellows are perfect in the great commanders' names, and they will learn you by rote where services were done: at such and such a sconce, at such a breach, at such a convoy; who came off bravely, who was shot, who disgraced; what terms the enemy stood on. And this they con perfectly in the phrase of war, which they trick up with new-tuned oaths. And what a beard of the general's cut and a horrid suit of the camp will do among foaming bottles and ale-washed wits is wonderful to be thought on. But you must learn to know such slanders of the age, or else you may be marvelously mistook.

FLUELLEN

I tell you what, Captain Gower: I do perceive he is not the man that he would gladly make show to the world he is. If I find a hole in his coat, I will tell him my mind. [Drum within] Hark you, the king is coming, and I must speak with him from the pridge.

Drum and Colors. Enter the King and his poor soldiers[, and Gloucester].

FLUELLEN

God pless your majesty.

KING HENRY

How now, Fluellen, cam'st thou from the bridge?

FLUELLEN

Ay, so please your majesty. The Duke of Exeter has very gallantly maintained the pridge. The French is gone off, look you, and there is gallant and most prave passages. Marry, th'athversary was have possession of the pridge, but he is enforced to retire, and the Duke of Exeter is master of the pridge. I can tell your majesty, the duke is a prave man.

KING HENRY

What men have you lost, Fluellen?

FLUELLEN

The perdition of th'athversary hath been very great, reasonable great. Marry, for my part I think the duke hath lost never a man, but one that is like to be executed for robbing a church: one Bardolph, if your majesty know the man. His face is all bubuckles, and whelks, and knobs, and flames afire, and his lips blows at his nose, and it is like a coal of fire, sometimes plue and sometimes red. But his nose is executed, and his fire's out.

KING HENRY

We would have all such offenders so cut off, and we give express charge that in our marches through the country there be nothing compelled from the villages, nothing taken but paid for, none of the French upbraided or abused in disdainful language. For when levity and cruelty play for a kingdom, the gentler gamester is the soonest winner.

Tucket. Enter Montjoy.

MONTJOY

You know me by my habit.

KING HENRY

Well then, I know thee. What shall I know of thee?

MONTJOY

My master's mind.

KING HENRY

Unfold it.

MONTJOY

Thus says my king: "Say thou to Harry of England, though we seemed dead, we did but sleep. Advantage is a better soldier than rashness. Tell him we could have rebuked him at Harfleur, but that we thought not good to bruise an injury till it were full ripe. Now we speak upon our cue, and our voice is imperial: England shall repent his folly, see his weakness, and admire our sufferance. Bid him therefore consider of his ransom, which must proportion the losses we have borne, the subjects we have lost, the disgrace we have digested, which in weight to re-answer his pettiness would bow under. For our losses, his exchequer is too poor; for th'effusion of our blood, the muster of his kingdom too faint a number; and for our disgrace, his own person kneeling at our feet but a weak and worthless satisfaction. To this add defiance, and tell him for conclusion he hath betrayed his followers, whose condemnation is pronounced." So far my king and master; so much my office.

KING HENRY

1057What is thy name? I know thy quality.

MONTJOY

1058Montjoy.

KING HENRY

1059Thou dost thy office fairly. Turn thee back
1060And tell thy king I do not seek him now,
1061But could be willing to march on to Calais
1062Without impeachment; for to say the sooth,
1063Though 'tis no wisdom to confess so much
1064Unto an enemy of craft and vantage,
1065My people are with sickness much enfeebled,
1066My numbers lessened, and those few I have
1067Almost no better than so many French,
1068Who when they were in health, I tell thee, herald,
1069I thought upon one pair of English legs
1070Did march three Frenchmen. Yet forgive me, God,
1071That I do brag thus; this your air of France
1072Hath blown that vice in me. I must repent.
1073Go therefore, tell thy master here I am.
1074My ransom is this frail and worthless trunk,
1075My army but a weak and sickly guard.
1076Yet, God before, tell him we will come on,
1077Though France himself and such another neighbor
1078Stand in our way.

[Gives money]

Stand in our way. There's for thy labor, Montjoy.
1079Go bid thy master well advise himself.
1080If we may pass, we will. If we be hindered,
1081We shall your tawny ground with your red blood
1082Discolor. And so, Montjoy, fare you well.
1083The sum of all our answer is but this:
1084We would not seek a battle as we are,
1085Nor as we are, we say, we will not shun it.
1086So tell your master.

MONTJOY

1087I shall deliver so. Thanks to your highness.

[Exit.]

GLOUCESTER

1088I hope they will not come upon us now.

KING HENRY

1089We are in God's hand, brother, not in theirs. —
1090March to the bridge. — It now draws toward night.
1091Beyond the river we'll encamp ourselves,
1092And on tomorrow bid them march away.

Exeunt.

[3.7]

Enter the Constable of France, the Lord Rambures, Orléans, [and the] Dauphin, with others.

CONSTABLE

Tut, I have the best armor of the world. Would it were day.

ORLÉANS

You have an excellent armor, but let my horse have his due.

CONSTABLE

It is the best horse of Europe.

ORLÉANS

Will it never be morning?

DAUPHIN

My lord of Orléans, and my lord high constable, you talk of horse and armor?

ORLÉANS

You are as well provided of both as any prince in the world.

DAUPHIN

What a long night is this! I will not change my horse with any that treads but on four pasterns. Ch'ha! He bounds from the earth as if his entrails were hairs: le cheval volant, the Pegasus, qui a les narines de feu! When I bestride him, I soar; I am a hawk. He trots the air. The earth sings when he touches it. The basest horn of his hoof is more musical than the pipe of Hermes.

ORLÉANS

He's of the color of the nutmeg.

DAUPHIN

And of the heat of the ginger. It is a beast for Perseus. He is pure air and fire, and the dull elements of earth and water never appear in him but only in patient stillness while his rider mounts him. He is indeed a horse, and all other jades you may call beasts.

CONSTABLE

Indeed, my lord, it is a most absolute and excellent horse.

DAUPHIN

It is the prince of palfreys. His neigh is like the bidding of a monarch, and his countenance enforces homage.

ORLÉANS

No more, cousin.

DAUPHIN

Nay, the man hath no wit that cannot from the rising of the lark to the lodging of the lamb vary deserved praise on my palfrey; it is a theme as fluent as the sea. Turn the sands into eloquent tongues and my horse is argument for them all. 'Tis a subject for a sovereign to reason on, and for a sovereign's sovereign to ride on, and for the world, familiar to us and unknown, to lay apart their particular functions and wonder at him. I once writ a sonnet in his praise, and began thus: "Wonder of nature —"

ORLÉANS

I have heard a sonnet begin so to one's mistress.

DAUPHIN

Then did they imitate that which I composed to my courser, for my horse is my mistress.

ORLÉANS

Your mistress bears well.

DAUPHIN

Me well, which is the prescript praise and perfection of a good and particular mistress.

CONSTABLE

Nay, for methought yesterday your mistress shrewdly shook your back.

DAUPHIN

So perhaps did yours.

CONSTABLE

Mine was not bridled.

DAUPHIN

Oh, then belike she was old and gentle, and you rode like a kern of Ireland, your French hose off, and in your strait strossers.

CONSTABLE

You have good judgment in horsemanship.

DAUPHIN

Be warned by me then: they that ride so and ride not warily fall into foul bogs. I had rather have my horse to my mistress.

CONSTABLE

I had as lief have my mistress a jade.

DAUPHIN

I tell thee, constable, my mistress wears his own hair.

CONSTABLE

I could make as true a boast as that if I had a sow to my mistress.

DAUPHIN

" Le chien est retourné à son propre vomissement, et la truie lavée au bourbier. " Thou makest use of anything.

CONSTABLE

Yet do I not use my horse for my mistress, or any such proverb so little kin to the purpose.

RAMBURES

My lord constable, the armor that I saw in your tent tonight: are those stars or suns upon it?

CONSTABLE

Stars, my lord.

DAUPHIN

Some of them will fall tomorrow, I hope.

CONSTABLE

And yet my sky shall not want.

DAUPHIN

That may be, for you bear a many superfluously, and 'twere more honor some were away.

CONSTABLE

Ev'n as your horse bears your praises, who would trot as well were some of your brags dismounted.

DAUPHIN

Would I were able to load him with his desert. Will it never be day? I will trot tomorrow a mile, and my way shall be paved with English faces.

CONSTABLE

I will not say so for fear I should be faced out of my way, but I would it were morning, for I would fain be about the ears of the English.

RAMBURES

Who will go to hazard with me for twenty prisoners?

CONSTABLE

You must first go yourself to hazard ere you have them.

DAUPHIN

'Tis midnight; I'll go arm myself.

Exit.

ORLÉANS

The dauphin longs for morning.

RAMBURES

He longs to eat the English.

CONSTABLE

I think he will eat all he kills.

ORLÉANS

By the white hand of my lady, he's a gallant prince.

CONSTABLE

Swear by her foot, that she may tread out the oath.

ORLÉANS

He is simply the most active gentleman of France.

CONSTABLE

Doing is activity, and he will still be doing.

ORLÉANS
